56:44 One should not wish to enjoy where one doth not contribute to the enjoyment. And one should not WISH to enjoy!
56:45 For enjoyment and innocence are the most bashful things. Neither like to be sought for. One should HAVE them,—but one should rather SEEK for guilt and pain!—
[SS 6]
56:46 O my brethren, he who is a firstling is ever sacrificed. Now, however, are we firstlings!
56:47 We all bleed on secret sacrificial altars, we all burn and broil in honour of ancient idols.
56:48 Our best is still young: this exciteth old palates. Our flesh is tender, our skin is only lambs' skin:—how could we not excite old idol-priests!
56:49 IN OURSELVES dwelleth he still, the old idol-priest, who broileth our best for his banquet. Ah, my brethren, how could firstlings fail to be sacrifices!
56:50 But so wisheth our type; and I love those who do not wish to preserve themselves, the down-going ones do I love with mine entire love: for they go beyond.—
[SS 7]
56:51 To be true—that CAN few be! And he who can, will not! Least of all, however, can the good be true.
56:52 Oh, those good ones! GOOD MEN NEVER SPEAK THE TRUTH. For the spirit, thus to be good, is a malady.
56:53 They yield, those good ones, they submit themselves; their heart repeateth, their soul obeyeth: HE, however, who obeyeth, DOTH NOT LISTEN TO HIMSELF!
56:54 All that is called evil by the good, must come together in order that one truth may be born. O my brethren, are ye also evil enough for THIS truth?
56:55 The daring venture, the prolonged distrust, the cruel Nay, the tedium, the cutting-into-the-quick—how seldom do THESE come together! Out of such seed, however—is truth produced!
56:56 BESIDE the bad conscience hath hitherto grown all KNOWLEDGE! Break up, break up, ye discerning ones, the old tables!
[SS 8]
56:57 When the water hath planks, when gangways and railings o'erspan the stream, verily, he is not believed who then saith: "All is in flux."
56:58 But even the simpletons contradict him. "What?" say the simpletons, "all in flux? Planks and railings are still OVER the stream!
56:59 "OVER the stream all is stable, all the values of things, the bridges and bearings, all 'good' and 'evil': these are all STABLE!"—
56:60 Cometh, however, the hard winter, the stream-tamer, then learn even the wittiest distrust, and verily, not only the simpletons then say: "Should not everything—STAND STILL?"
56:61 "Fundamentally standeth everything still"—that is an appropriate winter doctrine, good cheer for an unproductive period, a great comfort for winter-sleepers and fireside-loungers.
56:62 "Fundamentally standeth everything still"—: but CONTRARY thereto, preacheth the thawing wind!
56:63 The thawing wind, a bullock, which is no ploughing bullock—a furious bullock, a destroyer, which with angry horns breaketh the ice! The ice however—BREAKETH GANGWAYS!
56:64 O my brethren, is not everything AT PRESENT IN FLUX? Have not all railings and gangways fallen into the water? Who would still HOLD ON to "good" and "evil"?
56:65 "Woe to us! Hail to us! The thawing wind bloweth!"—Thus preach, my brethren, through all the streets!
[SS 9]
56:66 There is an old illusion—it is called good and evil. Around soothsayers and astrologers hath hitherto revolved the orbit of this illusion.
56:67 Once did one BELIEVE in soothsayers and astrologers; and THEREFORE did one believe, "Everything is fate: thou shalt, for thou must!"
56:68 Then again did one distrust all soothsayers and astrologers; and THEREFORE did one believe, "Everything is freedom: thou canst, for thou willest!"
56:69 O my brethren, concerning the stars and the future there hath hitherto been only illusion, and not knowledge; and THEREFORE concerning good and evil there hath hitherto been only illusion and not knowledge!
[SS 10]
56:70 "Thou shalt not rob! Thou shalt not slay!"—such precepts were once called holy; before them did one bow the knee and the head, and take off one's shoes.
56:71 But I ask you: Where have there ever been better robbers and slayers in the world than such holy precepts?
56:72 Is there not even in all life—robbing and slaying? And for such precepts to be called holy, was not TRUTH itself thereby—slain?
56:73 —Or was it a sermon of death that called holy what contradicted and dissuaded from life?—O my brethren, break up, break up for me the old tables!
[SS 11]
56:74 It is my sympathy with all the past that I see it is abandoned,—
56:75 —Abandoned to the favour, the spirit and the madness of every generation that cometh, and reinterpreteth all that hath been as its bridge!
56:76 A great potentate might arise, an artful prodigy, who with approval and disapproval could strain and constrain all the past, until it became for him a bridge, a harbinger, a herald, and a cock-crowing.
56:77 This however is the other danger, and mine other sympathy:—he who is of the populace, his thoughts go back to his grandfather,—with his grandfather, however, doth time cease.
56:78 Thus is all the past abandoned: for it might some day happen for the populace to become master, and drown all time in shallow waters.
56:79 Therefore, O my brethren, a NEW NOBILITY is needed, which shall be the adversary of all populace and potentate rule, and shall inscribe anew the word "noble" on new tables.
56:80 For many noble ones are needed, and many kinds of noble ones, FOR A NEW NOBILITY! Or, as I once said in parable: "That is just divinity, that there are Gods, but no God!"
[SS 12]
56:81 O my brethren, I consecrate you and point you to a new nobility: ye shall become procreators and cultivators and sowers of the future;—
56:82 —Verily, not to a nobility which ye could purchase like traders with traders' gold; for little worth is all that hath its price.
56:83 Let it not be your honour henceforth whence ye come, but whither ye go! Your Will and your feet which seek to surpass you—let these be your new honour!
56:84 Verily, not that ye have served a prince—of what account are princes now!—nor that ye have become a bulwark to that which standeth, that it may stand more firmly.
56:85 Not that your family have become courtly at courts, and that ye have learned—gay-coloured, like the flamingo—to stand long hours in shallow pools:
56:86 (For ABILITY-to-stand is a merit in courtiers; and all courtiers believe that unto blessedness after death pertaineth—PERMISSION-to-sit!)
56:87 Nor even that a Spirit called Holy, led your forefathers into promised lands, which I do not praise: for where the worst of all trees grew—the cross,—in that land there is nothing to praise!—
56:88 —And verily, wherever this "Holy Spirit" led its knights, always in such campaigns did—goats and geese, and wryheads and guyheads run FOREMOST!—
56:89 O my brethren, not backward shall your nobility gaze, but OUTWARD! Exiles shall ye be from all fatherlands and forefather-lands!
56:90 Your CHILDREN'S LAND shall ye love: let this love be your new nobility,—the undiscovered in the remotest seas! For it do I bid your sails search and search!
56:91 Unto your children shall ye MAKE AMENDS for being the children of your fathers: all the past shall ye THUS redeem! This new table do I place over you!
[SS 13]
56:92 "Why should one live? All is vain! To live—that is to thrash straw; to live—that is to burn oneself and yet not get warm."—
56:93 Such ancient babbling still passeth for "wisdom"; because it is old, however, and smelleth mustily, THEREFORE is it the more honoured. Even mould ennobleth.—
56:94 Children might thus speak: they SHUN the fire because it hath burnt them! There is much childishness in the old books of wisdom.
56:95 And he who ever "thrasheth straw," why should he be allowed to rail at thrashing! Such a fool one would have to muzzle!
56:96 Such persons sit down to the table and bring nothing with them, not even good hunger:—and then do they rail: "All is vain!"
56:97 But to eat and drink well, my brethren, is verily no vain art! Break up, break up for me the tables of the never-joyous ones!
[SS 14]
56:98 "To the clean are all things clean"—thus say the people. I, however, say unto you: To the swine all things become swinish!
56:99 Therefore preach the visionaries and bowed-heads (whose hearts are also bowed down): "The world itself is a filthy monster."
56:100 For these are all unclean spirits; especially those, however, who have no peace or rest, unless they see the world FROM THE BACKSIDE—the backworldsmen!
56:101 TO THOSE do I say it to the face, although it sound unpleasantly: the world resembleth man, in that it hath a backside,—SO MUCH is true!
56:102 There is in the world much filth: SO MUCH is true! But the world itself is not therefore a filthy monster!
56:103 There is wisdom in the fact that much in the world smelleth badly: loathing itself createth wings, and fountain-divining powers!
56:104 In the best there is still something to loathe; and the best is still something that must be surpassed!—
56:105 O my brethren, there is much wisdom in the fact that much filth is in the world!—
[SS 15]
56:106 Such sayings did I hear pious backworldsmen speak to their consciences, and verily without wickedness or guile,—although there is nothing more guileful in the world, or more wicked.
56:107 "Let the world be as it is! Raise not a finger against it!"
56:108 "Let whoever will choke and stab and skin and scrape the people: raise not a finger against it! Thereby will they learn to renounce the world."
56:109 "And thine own reason—this shalt thou thyself stifle and choke; for it is a reason of this world,—thereby wilt thou learn thyself to renounce the world."—
56:110 —Shatter, shatter, O my brethren, those old tables of the pious! Tatter the maxims of the world-maligners!—
[SS 16]
56:111 "He who learneth much unlearneth all violent cravings"—that do people now whisper to one another in all the dark lanes.
56:112 "Wisdom wearieth, nothing is worth while; thou shalt not crave!"—this new table found I hanging even in the public markets.
56:113 Break up for me, O my brethren, break up also that NEW table! The weary-o'-the-world put it up, and the preachers of death and the jailer: for lo, it is also a sermon for slavery:—
56:114 Because they learned badly and not the best, and everything too early and everything too fast; because they ATE badly: from thence hath resulted their ruined stomach;—
56:115 —For a ruined stomach, is their spirit: IT persuadeth to death! For verily, my brethren, the spirit IS a stomach!
56:116 Life is a well of delight, but to him in whom the ruined stomach speaketh, the father of affliction, all fountains are poisoned.
56:117 To discern: that is DELIGHT to the lion-willed! But he who hath become weary, is himself merely "willed"; with him play all the waves.
56:118 And such is always the nature of weak men: they lose themselves on their way. And at last asketh their weariness: "Why did we ever go on the way? All is indifferent!"
56:119 TO THEM soundeth it pleasant to have preached in their ears: "Nothing is worth while! Ye shall not will!" That, however, is a sermon for slavery.
56:120 O my brethren, a fresh blustering wind cometh Zarathustra unto all way-weary ones; many noses will he yet make sneeze!
56:121 Even through walls bloweth my free breath, and in into prisons and imprisoned spirits!
56:122 Willing emancipateth: for willing is creating: so do I teach. And ONLY for creating shall ye learn!
56:123 And also the learning shall ye LEARN only from me, the learning well!—He who hath ears let him hear!
[SS 17]
56:124 There standeth the boat—thither goeth it over, perhaps into vast nothingness—but who willeth to enter into this "Perhaps"?
56:125 None of you want to enter into the death-boat! How should ye then be WORLD-WEARY ones!
56:126 World-weary ones! And have not even withdrawn from the earth! Eager did I ever find you for the earth, amorous still of your own earth-weariness!
56:127 Not in vain doth your lip hang down:—a small worldly wish still sitteth thereon! And in your eye—floateth there not a cloudlet of unforgotten earthly bliss?
56:128 There are on the earth many good inventions, some useful, some pleasant: for their sake is the earth to be loved.
56:129 And many such good inventions are there, that they are like woman's breasts: useful at the same time, and pleasant.
56:130 Ye world-weary ones, however! Ye earth-idlers! You, shall one beat with stripes! With stripes shall one again make you sprightly limbs.
56:131 For if ye be not invalids, or decrepit creatures, of whom the earth is weary, then are ye sly sloths, or dainty, sneaking pleasure-cats. And if ye will not again RUN gaily, then shall ye—pass away!
56:132 To the incurable shall one not seek to be a physician: thus teacheth Zarathustra:—so shall ye pass away!
56:133 But more COURAGE is needed to make an end than to make a new verse: that do all physicians and poets know well.—
[SS 18]
56:134 O my brethren, there are tables which weariness framed, and tables which slothfulness framed, corrupt slothfulness: although they speak similarly, they want to be heard differently.—
56:135 See this languishing one! Only a span-breadth is he from his goal; but from weariness hath he lain down obstinately in the dust, this brave one!
56:136 From weariness yawneth he at the path, at the earth, at the goal, and at himself: not a step further will he go,—this brave one!
56:137 Now gloweth the sun upon him, and the dogs lick at his sweat: but he lieth there in his obstinacy and preferreth to languish:—
56:138 —A span-breadth from his goal, to languish! Verily, ye will have to drag him into his heaven by the hair of his head—this hero!
56:139 Better still that ye let him lie where he hath lain down, that sleep may come unto him, the comforter, with cooling patter-rain.
56:140 Let him lie, until of his own accord he awakeneth,—until of his own accord he repudiateth all weariness, and what weariness hath taught through him!
56:141 Only, my brethren, see that ye scare the dogs away from him, the idle skulkers, and all the swarming vermin:—
56:142 —All the swarming vermin of the "cultured," that—feast on the sweat of every hero!—
[SS 19]
56:143 I form circles around me and holy boundaries; ever fewer ascend with me ever higher mountains: I build a mountain-range out of ever holier mountains.—
56:144 But wherever ye would ascend with me, O my brethren, take care lest a PARASITE ascend with you!
56:145 A parasite: that is a reptile, a creeping, cringing reptile, that trieth to fatten on your infirm and sore places.
56:146 And THIS is its art: it divineth where ascending souls are weary, in your trouble and dejection, in your sensitive modesty, doth it build its loathsome nest.
56:147 Where the strong are weak, where the noble are all-too-gentle—there buildeth it its loathsome nest; the parasite liveth where the great have small sore places.
56:148 What is the highest of all species of being, and what is the lowest? The parasite is the lowest species; he, however, who is of the highest species feedeth most parasites.
56:149 For the soul which hath the longest ladder, and can go deepest down: how could there fail to be most parasites upon it?—
56:150 —The most comprehensive soul, which can run and stray and rove furthest in itself; the most necessary soul, which out of joy flingeth itself into chance:—
56:151 —The soul in Being, which plungeth into Becoming; the possessing soul, which SEEKETH to attain desire and longing:—
56:152 —The soul fleeing from itself, which overtaketh itself in the widest circuit; the wisest soul, unto which folly speaketh most sweetly:—
56:153 —The soul most self-loving, in which all things have their current and counter-current, their ebb and their flow:—oh, how could THE LOFTIEST SOUL fail to have the worst parasites?
[SS 20]
56:154 O my brethren, am I then cruel? But I say: What falleth, that shall one also push!
56:155 Everything of to-day—it falleth, it decayeth; who would preserve it! But I—I wish also to push it!
56:156 Know ye the delight which rolleth stones into precipitous depths?—Those men of to-day, see just how they roll into my depths!
56:157 A prelude am I to better players, O my brethren! An example! DO according to mine example!
56:158 And him whom ye do not teach to fly, teach I pray you—TO FALL FASTER!—
[SS 21]
56:159 I love the brave: but it is not enough to be a swordsman,—one must also know WHEREON to use swordsmanship!
56:160 And often is it greater bravery to keep quiet and pass by, that THEREBY one may reserve oneself for a worthier foe!
56:161 Ye shall only have foes to be hated; but not foes to be despised: ye must be proud of your foes. Thus have I already taught.
56:162 For the worthier foe, O my brethren, shall ye reserve yourselves: therefore must ye pass by many a one,—
56:163 —Especially many of the rabble, who din your ears with noise about people and peoples.
56:164 Keep your eye clear of their For and Against! There is there much right, much wrong: he who looketh on becometh wroth.
56:165 Therein viewing, therein hewing—they are the same thing: therefore depart into the forests and lay your sword to sleep!
56:166 Go YOUR ways! and let the people and peoples go theirs!—gloomy ways, verily, on which not a single hope glinteth any more!
56:167 Let there the trader rule, where all that still glittereth is—traders' gold. It is the time of kings no longer: that which now calleth itself the people is unworthy of kings.
56:168 See how these peoples themselves now do just like the traders: they pick up the smallest advantage out of all kinds of rubbish!
56:169 They lay lures for one another, they lure things out of one another,—that they call "good neighbourliness." O blessed remote period when a people said to itself: "I will be—MASTER over peoples!"
56:170 For, my brethren, the best shall rule, the best also WILLETH to rule! And where the teaching is different, there—the best is LACKING.
[SS 22]
56:171 If THEY had—bread for nothing, alas! for what would THEY cry! Their maintainment—that is their true entertainment; and they shall have it hard!
56:172 Beasts of prey, are they: in their "working"—there is even plundering, in their "earning"—there is even overreaching! Therefore shall they have it hard!
56:173 Better beasts of prey shall they thus become, subtler, cleverer, MORE MAN-LIKE: for man is the best beast of prey.
56:174 All the animals hath man already robbed of their virtues: that is why of all animals it hath been hardest for man.
56:175 Only the birds are still beyond him. And if man should yet learn to fly, alas! TO WHAT HEIGHT—would his rapacity fly!
[SS 23]
56:176 Thus would I have man and woman: fit for war, the one; fit for maternity, the other; both, however, fit for dancing with head and legs.
56:177 And lost be the day to us in which a measure hath not been danced. And false be every truth which hath not had laughter along with it!
[SS 24]
56:178 Your marriage-arranging: see that it be not a bad ARRANGING! Ye have arranged too hastily: so there FOLLOWETH therefrom—marriage-breaking!
56:179 And better marriage-breaking than marriage-bending, marriage-lying!—Thus spake a woman unto me: "Indeed, I broke the marriage, but first did the marriage break—me!"
56:180 The badly paired found I ever the most revengeful: they make every one suffer for it that they no longer run singly.
56:181 On that account want I the honest ones to say to one another: "We love each other: let us SEE TO IT that we maintain our love! Or shall our pledging be blundering?"
56:182 —"Give us a set term and a small marriage, that we may see if we are fit for the great marriage! It is a great matter always to be twain."
56:183 Thus do I counsel all honest ones; and what would be my love to the Superman, and to all that is to come, if I should counsel and speak otherwise!
56:184 Not only to propagate yourselves onwards but UPWARDS—thereto, O my brethren, may the garden of marriage help you!
[SS 25]
56:185 He who hath grown wise concerning old origins, lo, he will at last seek after the fountains of the future and new origins.—
56:186 O my brethren, not long will it be until NEW PEOPLES shall arise and new fountains shall rush down into new depths.
56:187 For the earthquake—it choketh up many wells, it causeth much languishing: but it bringeth also to light inner powers and secrets.
56:188 The earthquake discloseth new fountains. In the earthquake of old peoples new fountains burst forth.
56:189 And whoever calleth out: "Lo, here is a well for many thirsty ones, one heart for many longing ones, one will for many instruments":—around him collecteth a PEOPLE, that is to say, many attempting ones.
56:190 Who can command, who must obey—THAT IS THERE ATTEMPTED! Ah, with what long seeking and solving and failing and learning and re-attempting!
56:191 Human society: it is an attempt—so I teach—a long seeking: it seeketh however the ruler!—
56:192 —An attempt, my brethren! And NO "contract"! Destroy, I pray you, destroy that word of the soft-hearted and half-and-half!
[SS 26]
56:193 O my brethren! With whom lieth the greatest danger to the whole human future? Is it not with the good and just?—
56:194 —As those who say and feel in their hearts: "We already know what is good and just, we possess it also; woe to those who still seek thereafter!"
56:195 And whatever harm the wicked may do, the harm of the good is the harmfulest harm!
56:196 And whatever harm the world-maligners may do, the harm of the good is the harmfulest harm!
56:197 O my brethren, into the hearts of the good and just looked some one once on a time, who said: "They are the Pharisees." But people did not understand him.
56:198 The good and just themselves were not free to understand him; their spirit was imprisoned in their good conscience. The stupidity of the good is unfathomably wise.
56:199 It is the truth, however, that the good MUST be Pharisees—they have no choice!
56:200 The good MUST crucify him who deviseth his own virtue! That IS the truth!
56:201 The second one, however, who discovered their country—the country, heart and soil of the good and just,—it was he who asked: "Whom do they hate most?"
56:202 The CREATOR, hate they most, him who breaketh the tables and old values, the breaker,—him they call the law-breaker.
56:203 For the good—they CANNOT create; they are always the beginning of the end:—
56:204 —They crucify him who writeth new values on new tables, they sacrifice UNTO THEMSELVES the future—they crucify the whole human future!
56:205 The good—they have always been the beginning of the end.—
[SS 27]
56:206 O my brethren, have ye also understood this word? And what I once said of the "last man"?—
56:207 With whom lieth the greatest danger to the whole human future? Is it not with the good and just?
56:208 BREAK UP, BREAK UP, I PRAY YOU, THE GOOD AND JUST!—O my brethren, have ye understood also this word?
[SS 28]
56:209 Ye flee from me? Ye are frightened? Ye tremble at this word?
56:210 O my brethren, when I enjoined you to break up the good, and the tables of the good, then only did I embark man on his high seas.
56:211 And now only cometh unto him the great terror, the great outlook, the great sickness, the great nausea, the great sea-sickness.
56:212 False shores and false securities did the good teach you; in the lies of the good were ye born and bred. Everything hath been radically contorted and distorted by the good.
56:213 But he who discovered the country of "man," discovered also the country of "man's future." Now shall ye be sailors for me, brave, patient!
56:214 Keep yourselves up betimes, my brethren, learn to keep yourselves up! The sea stormeth: many seek to raise themselves again by you.
56:215 The sea stormeth: all is in the sea. Well! Cheer up! Ye old seaman-hearts!
56:216 What of fatherland! THITHER striveth our helm where our CHILDREN'S LAND is! Thitherwards, stormier than the sea, stormeth our great longing!—
[SS 29]
56:217 "Why so hard!"—said to the diamond one day the charcoal; "are we then not near relatives?"—
56:218 Why so soft? O my brethren; thus do _I_ ask you: are ye then not—my brethren?
56:219 Why so soft, so submissive and yielding? Why is there so much negation and abnegation in your hearts? Why is there so little fate in your looks?
56:220 And if ye will not be fates and inexorable ones, how can ye one day— conquer with me?
56:221 And if your hardness will not glance and cut and chip to pieces, how can ye one day—create with me?
56:222 For the creators are hard. And blessedness must it seem to you to press your hand upon millenniums as upon wax,—
56:223 —Blessedness to write upon the will of millenniums as upon brass,—harder than brass, nobler than brass. Entirely hard is only the noblest.
56:224 This new table, O my brethren, put I up over you: BECOME HARD!—
[SS 30]
56:225 O thou, my Will! Thou change of every need, MY needfulness! Preserve me from all small victories!
56:226 Thou fatedness of my soul, which I call fate! Thou In-me! Over-me! Preserve and spare me for one great fate!
56:227 And thy last greatness, my Will, spare it for thy last—that thou mayest be inexorable IN thy victory! Ah, who hath not succumbed to his victory!
56:228 Ah, whose eye hath not bedimmed in this intoxicated twilight! Ah, whose foot hath not faltered and forgotten in victory—how to stand!—
56:229 —That I may one day be ready and ripe in the great noontide: ready and ripe like the glowing ore, the lightning-bearing cloud, and the swelling milk-udder:—
56:230 —Ready for myself and for my most hidden Will: a bow eager for its arrow, an arrow eager for its star:—
56:231 —A star, ready and ripe in its noontide, glowing, pierced, blessed, by annihilating sun-arrows:—
56:232 —A sun itself, and an inexorable sun-will, ready for annihilation in victory!
56:233 O Will, thou change of every need, MY needfulness! Spare me for one great victory!—
56:234 Thus spake Zarathustra.


Chapter 57. The Convalescent

[SS 1]
57:1 One morning, not long after his return to his cave, Zarathustra sprang up from his couch like a madman, crying with a frightful voice, and acting as if some one still lay on the couch who did not wish to rise. Zarathustra's voice also resounded in such a manner that his animals came to him frightened, and out of all the neighbouring caves and lurking-places all the creatures slipped away—flying, fluttering, creeping or leaping, according to their variety of foot or wing. Zarathustra, however, spake these words:
57:2 Up, abysmal thought out of my depth! I am thy cock and morning dawn, thou overslept reptile: Up! Up! My voice shall soon crow thee awake!
57:3 Unbind the fetters of thine ears: listen! For I wish to hear thee! Up! Up! There is thunder enough to make the very graves listen!
57:4 And rub the sleep and all the dimness and blindness out of thine eyes! Hear me also with thine eyes: my voice is a medicine even for those born blind.
57:5 And once thou art awake, then shalt thou ever remain awake. It is not MY custom to awake great-grandmothers out of their sleep that I may bid them—sleep on!
57:6 Thou stirrest, stretchest thyself, wheezest? Up! Up! Not wheeze, shalt thou,—but speak unto me! Zarathustra calleth thee, Zarathustra the godless!
57:7 I, Zarathustra, the advocate of living, the advocate of suffering, the advocate of the circuit—thee do I call, my most abysmal thought!
57:8 Joy to me! Thou comest,—I hear thee! Mine abyss SPEAKETH, my lowest depth have I turned over into the light!
57:9 Joy to me! Come hither! Give me thy hand—ha! let be! aha!—Disgust, disgust, disgust—alas to me!
[SS 2]
57:10 Hardly, however, had Zarathustra spoken these words, when he fell down as one dead, and remained long as one dead. When however he again came to himself, then was he pale and trembling, and remained lying; and for long he would neither eat nor drink. This condition continued for seven days; his animals, however, did not leave him day nor night, except that the eagle flew forth to fetch food. And what it fetched and foraged, it laid on Zarathustra's couch: so that Zarathustra at last lay among yellow and red berries, grapes, rosy apples, sweet-smelling herbage, and pine-cones. At his feet, however, two lambs were stretched, which the eagle had with difficulty carried off from their shepherds.
57:11 At last, after seven days, Zarathustra raised himself upon his couch, took a rosy apple in his hand, smelt it and found its smell pleasant. Then did his animals think the time had come to speak unto him.
57:12 "O Zarathustra," said they, "now hast thou lain thus for seven days with heavy eyes: wilt thou not set thyself again upon thy feet?
57:13 Step out of thy cave: the world waiteth for thee as a garden. The wind playeth with heavy fragrance which seeketh for thee; and all brooks would like to run after thee.
57:14 All things long for thee, since thou hast remained alone for seven days—step forth out of thy cave! All things want to be thy physicians!
57:15 Did perhaps a new knowledge come to thee, a bitter, grievous knowledge? Like leavened dough layest thou, thy soul arose and swelled beyond all its bounds.—"
57:16 —O mine animals, answered Zarathustra, talk on thus and let me listen! It refresheth me so to hear your talk: where there is talk, there is the world as a garden unto me.
57:17 How charming it is that there are words and tones; are not words and tones rainbows and seeming bridges 'twixt the eternally separated?
57:18 To each soul belongeth another world; to each soul is every other soul a back-world.
57:19 Among the most alike doth semblance deceive most delightfully: for the smallest gap is most difficult to bridge over.
57:20 For me—how could there be an outside-of-me? There is no outside! But this we forget on hearing tones; how delightful it is that we forget!
57:21 Have not names and tones been given unto things that man may refresh himself with them? It is a beautiful folly, speaking; therewith danceth man over everything.
57:22 How lovely is all speech and all falsehoods of tones! With tones danceth our love on variegated rainbows.—
57:23 —"O Zarathustra," said then his animals, "to those who think like us, things all dance themselves: they come and hold out the hand and laugh and flee—and return.
57:24 Everything goeth, everything returneth; eternally rolleth the wheel of existence. Everything dieth, everything blossometh forth again; eternally runneth on the year of existence.
57:25 Everything breaketh, everything is integrated anew; eternally buildeth itself the same house of existence. All things separate, all things again greet one another; eternally true to itself remaineth the ring of existence.
57:26 Every moment beginneth existence, around every 'Here' rolleth the ball 'There.' The middle is everywhere. Crooked is the path of eternity."—
57:27 —O ye wags and barrel-organs! answered Zarathustra, and smiled once more, how well do ye know what had to be fulfilled in seven days:—
57:28 —And how that monster crept into my throat and choked me! But I bit off its head and spat it away from me.
57:29 And ye—ye have made a lyre-lay out of it? Now, however, do I lie here, still exhausted with that biting and spitting-away, still sick with mine own salvation.
57:30 AND YE LOOKED ON AT IT ALL? O mine animals, are ye also cruel? Did ye like to look at my great pain as men do? For man is the cruellest animal.
57:31 At tragedies, bull-fights, and crucifixions hath he hitherto been happiest on earth; and when he invented his hell, behold, that was his heaven on earth.
57:32 When the great man crieth—: immediately runneth the little man thither, and his tongue hangeth out of his mouth for very lusting. He, however, calleth it his "pity."
57:33 The little man, especially the poet—how passionately doth he accuse life in words! Hearken to him, but do not fail to hear the delight which is in all accusation!
57:34 Such accusers of life—them life overcometh with a glance of the eye. "Thou lovest me?" saith the insolent one; "wait a little, as yet have I no time for thee."
57:35 Towards himself man is the cruellest animal; and in all who call themselves "sinners" and "bearers of the cross" and "penitents," do not overlook the voluptuousness in their plaints and accusations!
57:36 And I myself—do I thereby want to be man's accuser? Ah, mine animals, this only have I learned hitherto, that for man his baddest is necessary for his best,—
57:37 —That all that is baddest is the best POWER, and the hardest stone for the highest creator; and that man must become better AND badder:—
57:38 Not to THIS torture-stake was I tied, that I know man is bad,—but I cried, as no one hath yet cried:
57:39 "Ah, that his baddest is so very small! Ah, that his best is so very small!"
57:40 The great disgust at man—IT strangled me and had crept into my throat: and what the soothsayer had presaged: "All is alike, nothing is worth while, knowledge strangleth."
57:41 A long twilight limped on before me, a fatally weary, fatally intoxicated sadness, which spake with yawning mouth.
57:42 "Eternally he returneth, the man of whom thou art weary, the small man"—so yawned my sadness, and dragged its foot and could not go to sleep.
57:43 A cavern, became the human earth to me; its breast caved in; everything living became to me human dust and bones and mouldering past.
57:44 My sighing sat on all human graves, and could no longer arise: my sighing and questioning croaked and choked, and gnawed and nagged day and night:
57:45 —"Ah, man returneth eternally! The small man returneth eternally!"
57:46 Naked had I once seen both of them, the greatest man and the smallest man: all too like one another—all too human, even the greatest man!
57:47 All too small, even the greatest man!—that was my disgust at man! And the eternal return also of the smallest man!—that was my disgust at all existence!
57:48 Ah, Disgust! Disgust! Disgust!—Thus spake Zarathustra, and sighed and shuddered; for he remembered his sickness. Then did his animals prevent him from speaking further.
57:49 "Do not speak further, thou convalescent!"—so answered his animals, "but go out where the world waiteth for thee like a garden.
57:50 Go out unto the roses, the bees, and the flocks of doves! Especially, however, unto the singing birds, to learn SINGING from them!
57:51 For singing is for the convalescent; the sound ones may talk. And when the sound also want songs, then want they other songs than the convalescent."
57:52 —"O ye wags and barrel-organs, do be silent!" answered Zarathustra, and smiled at his animals. "How well ye know what consolation I devised for myself in seven days!
57:53 That I have to sing once more—THAT consolation did I devise for myself, and THIS convalescence: would ye also make another lyre-lay thereof?"
57:54 —"Do not talk further," answered his animals once more; "rather, thou convalescent, prepare for thyself first a lyre, a new lyre!
57:55 For behold, O Zarathustra! For thy new lays there are needed new lyres.
57:56 Sing and bubble over, O Zarathustra, heal thy soul with new lays: that thou mayest bear thy great fate, which hath not yet been any one's fate!
57:57 For thine animals know it well, O Zarathustra, who thou art and must become: behold, THOU ART THE TEACHER OF THE ETERNAL RETURN,—that is now THY fate!
57:58 That thou must be the first to teach this teaching—how could this great fate not be thy greatest danger and infirmity!
57:59 Behold, we know what thou teachest: that all things eternally return, and ourselves with them, and that we have already existed times without number, and all things with us.
57:60 Thou teachest that there is a great year of Becoming, a prodigy of a great year; it must, like a sand-glass, ever turn up anew, that it may anew run down and run out:—
57:61 —So that all those years are like one another in the greatest and also in the smallest, so that we ourselves, in every great year, are like ourselves in the greatest and also in the smallest.
57:62 And if thou wouldst now die, O Zarathustra, behold, we know also how thou wouldst then speak to thyself:—but thine animals beseech thee not to die yet!
57:63 Thou wouldst speak, and without trembling, buoyant rather with bliss, for a great weight and worry would be taken from thee, thou patientest one!—
57:64 'Now do I die and disappear,' wouldst thou say, 'and in a moment I am nothing. Souls are as mortal as bodies.
57:65 But the plexus of causes returneth in which I am intertwined,—it will again create me! I myself pertain to the causes of the eternal return.
57:66 I come again with this sun, with this earth, with this eagle, with this serpent—NOT to a new life, or a better life, or a similar life:
57:67 —I come again eternally to this identical and selfsame life, in its greatest and its smallest, to teach again the eternal return of all things,—
57:68 —To speak again the word of the great noontide of earth and man, to announce again to man the Superman.
57:69 I have spoken my word. I break down by my word: so willeth mine eternal fate—as announcer do I succumb!
57:70 The hour hath now come for the down-goer to bless himself. Thus—ENDETH Zarathustra's down-going.'"—
57:71 When the animals had spoken these words they were silent and waited, so that Zarathustra might say something to them: but Zarathustra did not hear that they were silent. On the contrary, he lay quietly with closed eyes like a person sleeping, although he did not sleep; for he communed just then with his soul. The serpent, however, and the eagle, when they found him silent in such wise, respected the great stillness around him, and prudently retired.


Chapter 58. The Great Longing

58:1 O my soul, I have taught thee to say "to-day" as "once on a time" and "formerly," and to dance thy measure over every Here and There and Yonder.
58:2 O my soul, I delivered thee from all by-places, I brushed down from thee dust and spiders and twilight.
58:3 O my soul, I washed the petty shame and the by-place virtue from thee, and persuaded thee to stand naked before the eyes of the sun.
58:4 With the storm that is called "spirit" did I blow over thy surging sea; all clouds did I blow away from it; I strangled even the strangler called "sin."
58:5 O my soul, I gave thee the right to say Nay like the storm, and to say Yea as the open heaven saith Yea: calm as the light remainest thou, and now walkest through denying storms.
58:6 O my soul, I restored to thee liberty over the created and the uncreated; and who knoweth, as thou knowest, the voluptuousness of the future?
58:7 O my soul, I taught thee the contempt which doth not come like worm-eating, the great, the loving contempt, which loveth most where it contemneth most.
58:8 O my soul, I taught thee so to persuade that thou persuadest even the grounds themselves to thee: like the sun, which persuadeth even the sea to its height.
58:9 O my soul, I have taken from thee all obeying and knee-bending and homage-paying; I have myself given thee the names, "Change of need" and "Fate."
58:10 O my soul, I have given thee new names and gay-coloured playthings, I have called thee "Fate" and "the Circuit of circuits" and "the Navel-string of time" and "the Azure bell."
58:11 O my soul, to thy domain gave I all wisdom to drink, all new wines, and also all immemorially old strong wines of wisdom.
58:12 O my soul, every sun shed I upon thee, and every night and every silence and every longing:—then grewest thou up for me as a vine.
58:13 O my soul, exuberant and heavy dost thou now stand forth, a vine with swelling udders and full clusters of brown golden grapes:—
58:14 —Filled and weighted by thy happiness, waiting from superabundance, and yet ashamed of thy waiting.
58:15 O my soul, there is nowhere a soul which could be more loving and more comprehensive and more extensive! Where could future and past be closer together than with thee?
58:16 O my soul, I have given thee everything, and all my hands have become empty by thee:—and now! Now sayest thou to me, smiling and full of melancholy: "Which of us oweth thanks?—
58:17 —Doth the giver not owe thanks because the receiver received? Is bestowing not a necessity? Is receiving not—pitying?"—
58:18 O my soul, I understand the smiling of thy melancholy: thine over-abundance itself now stretcheth out longing hands!
58:19 Thy fulness looketh forth over raging seas, and seeketh and waiteth: the longing of over-fulness looketh forth from the smiling heaven of thine eyes!
58:20 And verily, O my soul! Who could see thy smiling and not melt into tears? The angels themselves melt into tears through the over-graciousness of thy smiling.
58:21 Thy graciousness and over-graciousness, is it which will not complain and weep: and yet, O my soul, longeth thy smiling for tears, and thy trembling mouth for sobs.
58:22 "Is not all weeping complaining? And all complaining, accusing?" Thus speakest thou to thyself; and therefore, O my soul, wilt thou rather smile than pour forth thy grief—
58:23 —Than in gushing tears pour forth all thy grief concerning thy fulness, and concerning the craving of the vine for the vintager and vintage-knife!
58:24 But wilt thou not weep, wilt thou not weep forth thy purple melancholy, then wilt thou have to SING, O my soul!—Behold, I smile myself, who foretell thee this:
58:25 —Thou wilt have to sing with passionate song, until all seas turn calm to hearken unto thy longing,—
58:26 —Until over calm longing seas the bark glideth, the golden marvel, around the gold of which all good, bad, and marvellous things frisk:—
58:27 —Also many large and small animals, and everything that hath light marvellous feet, so that it can run on violet-blue paths,—
58:28 —Towards the golden marvel, the spontaneous bark, and its master: he, however, is the vintager who waiteth with the diamond vintage-knife,—
58:29 —Thy great deliverer, O my soul, the nameless one—for whom future songs only will find names! And verily, already hath thy breath the fragrance of future songs,—
58:30 —Already glowest thou and dreamest, already drinkest thou thirstily at all deep echoing wells of consolation, already reposeth thy melancholy in the bliss of future songs!—
58:31 O my soul, now have I given thee all, and even my last possession, and all my hands have become empty by thee:—THAT I BADE THEE SING, behold, that was my last thing to give!
58:32 That I bade thee sing,—say now, say: WHICH of us now—oweth thanks?— Better still, however: sing unto me, sing, O my soul! And let me thank thee!—
58:33 Thus spake Zarathustra.


Chapter 59. The Second Dance-song

[SS 1]
59:1 "Into thine eyes gazed I lately, O Life: gold saw I gleam in thy night-eyes,—my heart stood still with delight:
59:2 —A golden bark saw I gleam on darkened waters, a sinking, drinking, reblinking, golden swing-bark!
59:3 At my dance-frantic foot, dost thou cast a glance, a laughing, questioning, melting, thrown glance:
59:4 Twice only movedst thou thy rattle with thy little hands—then did my feet swing with dance-fury.—
59:5 My heels reared aloft, my toes they hearkened,—thee they would know: hath not the dancer his ear—in his toe!
59:6 Unto thee did I spring: then fledst thou back from my bound; and towards me waved thy fleeing, flying tresses round!
59:7 Away from thee did I spring, and from thy snaky tresses: then stoodst thou there half-turned, and in thine eye caresses.
59:8 With crooked glances—dost thou teach me crooked courses; on crooked courses learn my feet—crafty fancies!
59:9 I fear thee near, I love thee far; thy flight allureth me, thy seeking secureth me:—I suffer, but for thee, what would I not gladly bear!
59:10 For thee, whose coldness inflameth, whose hatred misleadeth, whose flight enchaineth, whose mockery—pleadeth:
59:11 —Who would not hate thee, thou great bindress, inwindress, temptress, seekress, findress! Who would not love thee, thou innocent, impatient, wind-swift, child-eyed sinner!
59:12 Whither pullest thou me now, thou paragon and tomboy? And now foolest thou me fleeing; thou sweet romp dost annoy!
59:13 I dance after thee, I follow even faint traces lonely. Where art thou? Give me thy hand! Or thy finger only!
59:14 Here are caves and thickets: we shall go astray!—Halt! Stand still! Seest thou not owls and bats in fluttering fray?
59:15 Thou bat! Thou owl! Thou wouldst play me foul? Where are we? From the dogs hast thou learned thus to bark and howl.
59:16 Thou gnashest on me sweetly with little white teeth; thine evil eyes shoot out upon me, thy curly little mane from underneath!
59:17 This is a dance over stock and stone: I am the hunter,—wilt thou be my hound, or my chamois anon?
59:18 Now beside me! And quickly, wickedly springing! Now up! And over!—Alas! I have fallen myself overswinging!
59:19 Oh, see me lying, thou arrogant one, and imploring grace! Gladly would I walk with thee—in some lovelier place!
59:20 —In the paths of love, through bushes variegated, quiet, trim! Or there along the lake, where gold-fishes dance and swim!
59:21 Thou art now aweary? There above are sheep and sun-set stripes: is it not sweet to sleep—the shepherd pipes?
59:22 Thou art so very weary? I carry thee thither; let just thine arm sink! And art thou thirsty—I should have something; but thy mouth would not like it to drink!—
59:23 —Oh, that cursed, nimble, supple serpent and lurking-witch! Where art thou gone? But in my face do I feel through thy hand, two spots and red blotches itch!
59:24 I am verily weary of it, ever thy sheepish shepherd to be. Thou witch, if I have hitherto sung unto thee, now shalt THOU—cry unto me!
59:25 To the rhythm of my whip shalt thou dance and cry! I forget not my whip?—Not I!"—
[SS 2]
59:26 Then did Life answer me thus, and kept thereby her fine ears closed:
59:27 "O Zarathustra! Crack not so terribly with thy whip! Thou knowest surely that noise killeth thought,—and just now there came to me such delicate thoughts.
59:28 We are both of us genuine ne'er-do-wells and ne'er-do-ills. Beyond good and evil found we our island and our green meadow—we two alone! Therefore must we be friendly to each other!
59:29 And even should we not love each other from the bottom of our hearts,—must we then have a grudge against each other if we do not love each other perfectly?
59:30 And that I am friendly to thee, and often too friendly, that knowest thou: and the reason is that I am envious of thy Wisdom. Ah, this mad old fool, Wisdom!
59:31 If thy Wisdom should one day run away from thee, ah! then would also my love run away from thee quickly."—
59:32 Thereupon did Life look thoughtfully behind and around, and said softly: "O Zarathustra, thou art not faithful enough to me!
59:33 Thou lovest me not nearly so much as thou sayest; I know thou thinkest of soon leaving me.
59:34 There is an old heavy, heavy, booming-clock: it boometh by night up to thy cave:—
59:35 —When thou hearest this clock strike the hours at midnight, then thinkest thou between one and twelve thereon—
59:36 —Thou thinkest thereon, O Zarathustra, I know it—of soon leaving me!"—
59:37 "Yea," answered I, hesitatingly, "but thou knowest it also"—And I said something into her ear, in amongst her confused, yellow, foolish tresses.
59:38 "Thou KNOWEST that, O Zarathustra? That knoweth no one—"
59:39 And we gazed at each other, and looked at the green meadow o'er which the cool evening was just passing, and we wept together.—Then, however, was Life dearer unto me than all my Wisdom had ever been.—
59:40 Thus spake Zarathustra.
[SS 3]
59:41 _One!_
59:42 O man! Take heed!
59:43 _Two!_
59:44 What saith deep midnight's voice indeed?
59:45 _Three!_
59:46 "I slept my sleep—
59:47 _Four!_
59:48 "From deepest dream I've woke and plead:—
59:49 _Five!_
59:50 "The world is deep,
59:51 _Six!_
59:52 "And deeper than the day could read.
59:53 _Seven!_
59:54 "Deep is its woe—
59:55 _Eight!_
59:56 "Joy—deeper still than grief can be:
59:57 _Nine!_
59:58 "Woe saith: Hence! Go!
59:59 _Ten!_
59:60 "But joys all want eternity—
59:61 _Eleven!_
59:62 "Want deep profound eternity!"
59:63 _Twelve!_


Chapter 60. The Seven Seals

60:1 (OR THE YE-A AND AMEN LAY.)
[SS 1]
60:2 If I be a diviner and full of the divining spirit which wandereth on high mountain-ridges, 'twixt two seas,—
60:3 Wandereth 'twixt the past and the future as a heavy cloud—hostile to sultry plains, and to all that is weary and can neither die nor live:
60:4 Ready for lightning in its dark bosom, and for the redeeming flash of light, charged with lightnings which say Yea! which laugh Yea! ready for divining flashes of lightning:—
60:5 —Blessed, however, is he who is thus charged! And verily, long must he hang like a heavy tempest on the mountain, who shall one day kindle the light of the future!—
60:6 Oh, how could I not be ardent for Eternity and for the marriage-ring of rings—the ring of the return?
60:7 Never yet have I found the woman by whom I should like to have children, unless it be this woman whom I love: for I love thee, O Eternity!
60:8 FOR I LOVE THEE, O ETERNITY!
[SS 2]
60:9 If ever my wrath hath burst graves, shifted landmarks, or rolled old shattered tables into precipitous depths:
60:10 If ever my scorn hath scattered mouldered words to the winds, and if I have come like a besom to cross-spiders, and as a cleansing wind to old charnel-houses:
60:11 If ever I have sat rejoicing where old Gods lie buried, world-blessing, world-loving, beside the monuments of old world-maligners:—
60:12 —For even churches and Gods'-graves do I love, if only heaven looketh through their ruined roofs with pure eyes; gladly do I sit like grass and red poppies on ruined churches—
60:13 Oh, how could I not be ardent for Eternity, and for the marriage-ring of rings—the ring of the return?
60:14 Never yet have I found the woman by whom I should like to have children, unless it be this woman whom I love: for I love thee, O Eternity!
60:15 FOR I LOVE THEE, O ETERNITY!
[SS 3]
60:16 If ever a breath hath come to me of the creative breath, and of the heavenly necessity which compelleth even chances to dance star-dances:
60:17 If ever I have laughed with the laughter of the creative lightning, to which the long thunder of the deed followeth, grumblingly, but obediently:
60:18 If ever I have played dice with the Gods at the divine table of the earth, so that the earth quaked and ruptured, and snorted forth fire-streams:—
60:19 —For a divine table is the earth, and trembling with new creative dictums and dice-casts of the Gods:
60:20 Oh, how could I not be ardent for Eternity, and for the marriage-ring of rings—the ring of the return?
60:21 Never yet have I found the woman by whom I should like to have children, unless it be this woman whom I love: for I love thee, O Eternity!
60:22 FOR I LOVE THEE, O ETERNITY!
[SS 4]
60:23 If ever I have drunk a full draught of the foaming spice- and confection-bowl in which all things are well mixed:
60:24 If ever my hand hath mingled the furthest with the nearest, fire with spirit, joy with sorrow, and the harshest with the kindest:
60:25 If I myself am a grain of the saving salt which maketh everything in the confection-bowl mix well:—
60:26 —For there is a salt which uniteth good with evil; and even the evilest is worthy, as spicing and as final over-foaming:—
60:27 Oh, how could I not be ardent for Eternity, and for the marriage-ring of rings—the ring of the return?
60:28 Never yet have I found the woman by whom I should like to have children, unless it be this woman whom I love: for I love thee, O Eternity!
60:29 FOR I LOVE THEE, O ETERNITY!
[SS 5]
60:30 If I be fond of the sea, and all that is sealike, and fondest of it when it angrily contradicteth me:
60:31 If the exploring delight be in me, which impelleth sails to the undiscovered, if the seafarer's delight be in my delight:
60:32 If ever my rejoicing hath called out: "The shore hath vanished,—now hath fallen from me the last chain—
60:33 The boundless roareth around me, far away sparkle for me space and time,—well! cheer up! old heart!"—
60:34 Oh, how could I not be ardent for Eternity, and for the marriage-ring of rings—the ring of the return?
60:35 Never yet have I found the woman by whom I should like to have children, unless it be this woman whom I love: for I love thee, O Eternity!
60:36 FOR I LOVE THEE, O ETERNITY!
[SS 6]
60:37 If my virtue be a dancer's virtue, and if I have often sprung with both feet into golden-emerald rapture:
60:38 If my wickedness be a laughing wickedness, at home among rose-banks and hedges of lilies:
60:39 —For in laughter is all evil present, but it is sanctified and absolved by its own bliss:—
60:40 And if it be my Alpha and Omega that everything heavy shall become light, every body a dancer, and every spirit a bird: and verily, that is my Alpha and Omega!—
60:41 Oh, how could I not be ardent for Eternity, and for the marriage-ring of rings—the ring of the return?
60:42 Never yet have I found the woman by whom I should like to have children, unless it be this woman whom I love: for I love thee, O Eternity!
60:43 FOR I LOVE THEE, O ETERNITY!
[SS 7]
60:44 If ever I have spread out a tranquil heaven above me, and have flown into mine own heaven with mine own pinions:
60:45 If I have swum playfully in profound luminous distances, and if my freedom's avian wisdom hath come to me:—
60:46 —Thus however speaketh avian wisdom:—"Lo, there is no above and no below! Throw thyself about,—outward, backward, thou light one! Sing! speak no more!
60:47 —Are not all words made for the heavy? Do not all words lie to the light ones? Sing! speak no more!"—
60:48 Oh, how could I not be ardent for Eternity, and for the marriage-ring of rings—the ring of the return?
60:49 Never yet have I found the woman by whom I should like to have children, unless it be this woman whom I love: for I love thee, O Eternity!
60:50 FOR I LOVE THEE, O ETERNITY!



PART 4


Chapter 61. The Honey Sacrifice

61:1 —And again passed moons and years over Zarathustra's soul, and he heeded it not; his hair, however, became white. One day when he sat on a stone in front of his cave, and gazed calmly into the distance—one there gazeth out on the sea, and away beyond sinuous abysses,—then went his animals thoughtfully round about him, and at last set themselves in front of him.
61:2 "O Zarathustra," said they, "gazest thou out perhaps for thy happiness?"—"Of what account is my happiness!" answered he, "I have long ceased to strive any more for happiness, I strive for my work."—"O Zarathustra," said the animals once more, "that sayest thou as one who hath overmuch of good things. Liest thou not in a sky-blue lake of happiness?"—"Ye wags," answered Zarathustra, and smiled, "how well did ye choose the simile! But ye know also that my happiness is heavy, and not like a fluid wave of water: it presseth me and will not leave me, and is like molten pitch."—
61:3 Then went his animals again thoughtfully around him, and placed themselves once more in front of him. "O Zarathustra," said they, "it is consequently FOR THAT REASON that thou thyself always becometh yellower and darker, although thy hair looketh white and flaxen? Lo, thou sittest in thy pitch!"—"What do ye say, mine animals?" said Zarathustra, laughing; "verily I reviled when I spake of pitch. As it happeneth with me, so is it with all fruits that turn ripe. It is the HONEY in my veins that maketh my blood thicker, and also my soul stiller."—"So will it be, O Zarathustra," answered his animals, and pressed up to him; "but wilt thou not to-day ascend a high mountain? The air is pure, and to-day one seeth more of the world than ever."—"Yea, mine animals," answered he, "ye counsel admirably and according to my heart: I will to-day ascend a high mountain! But see that honey is there ready to hand, yellow, white, good, ice-cool, golden-comb-honey. For know that when aloft I will make the honey sacrifice."—
61:4 When Zarathustra, however, was aloft on the summit, he sent his animals home that had accompanied him, and found that he was now alone:—then he laughed from the bottom of his heart, looked around him, and spake thus:
61:5 That I spake of sacrifices and honey-sacrifices, it was merely a ruse in talking and verily, a useful folly! Here aloft can I now speak freer than in front of mountain-caves and anchorites' domestic animals.
61:6 What to sacrifice! I squander what is given me, a squanderer with a thousand hands: how could I call that—sacrificing?
61:7 And when I desired honey I only desired bait, and sweet mucus and mucilage, for which even the mouths of growling bears, and strange, sulky, evil birds, water:
61:8 —The best bait, as huntsmen and fishermen require it. For if the world be as a gloomy forest of animals, and a pleasure-ground for all wild huntsmen, it seemeth to me rather—and preferably—a fathomless, rich sea;
61:9 —A sea full of many-hued fishes and crabs, for which even the Gods might long, and might be tempted to become fishers in it, and casters of nets,—so rich is the world in wonderful things, great and small!
61:10 Especially the human world, the human sea:—towards IT do I now throw out my golden angle-rod and say: Open up, thou human abyss!
61:11 Open up, and throw unto me thy fish and shining crabs! With my best bait shall I allure to myself to-day the strangest human fish!
61:12 —My happiness itself do I throw out into all places far and wide 'twixt orient, noontide, and occident, to see if many human fish will not learn to hug and tug at my happiness;—
61:13 Until, biting at my sharp hidden hooks, they have to come up unto MY height, the motleyest abyss-groundlings, to the wickedest of all fishers of men.
61:14 For THIS am I from the heart and from the beginning—drawing, hither-drawing, upward-drawing, upbringing; a drawer, a trainer, a training-master, who not in vain counselled himself once on a time: "Become what thou art!"
61:15 Thus may men now come UP to me; for as yet do I await the signs that it is time for my down-going; as yet do I not myself go down, as I must do, amongst men.
61:16 Therefore do I here wait, crafty and scornful upon high mountains, no impatient one, no patient one; rather one who hath even unlearnt patience,—because he no longer "suffereth."
61:17 For my fate giveth me time: it hath forgotten me perhaps? Or doth it sit behind a big stone and catch flies?
61:18 And verily, I am well disposed to mine eternal fate, because it doth not hound and hurry me, but leaveth me time for merriment and mischief; so that I have to-day ascended this high mountain to catch fish.
61:19 Did ever any one catch fish upon high mountains? And though it be a folly what I here seek and do, it is better so than that down below I should become solemn with waiting, and green and yellow—
61:20 —A posturing wrath-snorter with waiting, a holy howl-storm from the mountains, an impatient one that shouteth down into the valleys: "Hearken, else I will scourge you with the scourge of God!"
61:21 Not that I would have a grudge against such wrathful ones on that account: they are well enough for laughter to me! Impatient must they now be, those big alarm-drums, which find a voice now or never!
61:22 Myself, however, and my fate—we do not talk to the Present, neither do we talk to the Never: for talking we have patience and time and more than time. For one day must it yet come, and may not pass by.
61:23 What must one day come and may not pass by? Our great Hazar, that is to say, our great, remote human-kingdom, the Zarathustra-kingdom of a thousand years—
61:24 How remote may such "remoteness" be? What doth it concern me? But on that account it is none the less sure unto me—, with both feet stand I secure on this ground;
61:25 —On an eternal ground, on hard primary rock, on this highest, hardest, primary mountain-ridge, unto which all winds come, as unto the storm-parting, asking Where? and Whence? and Whither?
61:26 Here laugh, laugh, my hearty, healthy wickedness! From high mountains cast down thy glittering scorn-laughter! Allure for me with thy glittering the finest human fish!
61:27 And whatever belongeth unto ME in all seas, my in-and-for-me in all things—fish THAT out for me, bring THAT up to me: for that do I wait, the wickedest of all fish-catchers.
61:28 Out! out! my fishing-hook! In and down, thou bait of my happiness! Drip thy sweetest dew, thou honey of my heart! Bite, my fishing-hook, into the belly of all black affliction!
61:29 Look out, look out, mine eye! Oh, how many seas round about me, what dawning human futures! And above me—what rosy red stillness! What unclouded silence!


Chapter 62. The Cry of Distress

62:1 The next day sat Zarathustra again on the stone in front of his cave, whilst his animals roved about in the world outside to bring home new food,—also new honey: for Zarathustra had spent and wasted the old honey to the very last particle. When he thus sat, however, with a stick in his hand, tracing the shadow of his figure on the earth, and reflecting—verily! not upon himself and his shadow,—all at once he startled and shrank back: for he saw another shadow beside his own. And when he hastily looked around and stood up, behold, there stood the soothsayer beside him, the same whom he had once given to eat and drink at his table, the proclaimer of the great weariness, who taught: "All is alike, nothing is worth while, the world is without meaning, knowledge strangleth." But his face had changed since then; and when Zarathustra looked into his eyes, his heart was startled once more: so much evil announcement and ashy-grey lightnings passed over that countenance.
62:2 The soothsayer, who had perceived what went on in Zarathustra's soul, wiped his face with his hand, as if he would wipe out the impression; the same did also Zarathustra. And when both of them had thus silently composed and strengthened themselves, they gave each other the hand, as a token that they wanted once more to recognise each other.
62:3 "Welcome hither," said Zarathustra, "thou soothsayer of the great weariness, not in vain shalt thou once have been my messmate and guest. Eat and drink also with me to-day, and forgive it that a cheerful old man sitteth with thee at table!"—"A cheerful old man?" answered the soothsayer, shaking his head, "but whoever thou art, or wouldst be, O Zarathustra, thou hast been here aloft the longest time,—in a little while thy bark shall no longer rest on dry land!"—"Do I then rest on dry land?"—asked Zarathustra, laughing.—"The waves around thy mountain," answered the soothsayer, "rise and rise, the waves of great distress and affliction: they will soon raise thy bark also and carry thee away."—Thereupon was Zarathustra silent and wondered.—"Dost thou still hear nothing?" continued the soothsayer: "doth it not rush and roar out of the depth?"—Zarathustra was silent once more and listened: then heard he a long, long cry, which the abysses threw to one another and passed on; for none of them wished to retain it: so evil did it sound.
62:4 "Thou ill announcer," said Zarathustra at last, "that is a cry of distress, and the cry of a man; it may come perhaps out of a black sea. But what doth human distress matter to me! My last sin which hath been reserved for me,—knowest thou what it is called?"
62:5 —"PITY!" answered the soothsayer from an overflowing heart, and raised both his hands aloft—"O Zarathustra, I have come that I may seduce thee to thy last sin!"—
62:6 And hardly had those words been uttered when there sounded the cry once more, and longer and more alarming than before—also much nearer. "Hearest thou? Hearest thou, O Zarathustra?" called out the soothsayer, "the cry concerneth thee, it calleth thee: Come, come, come; it is time, it is the highest time!"—
62:7 Zarathustra was silent thereupon, confused and staggered; at last he asked, like one who hesitateth in himself: "And who is it that there calleth me?"
62:8 "But thou knowest it, certainly," answered the soothsayer warmly, "why dost thou conceal thyself? It is THE HIGHER MAN that crieth for thee!"
62:9 "The higher man?" cried Zarathustra, horror-stricken: "what wanteth HE? What wanteth HE? The higher man! What wanteth he here?"—and his skin covered with perspiration.
62:10 The soothsayer, however, did not heed Zarathustra's alarm, but listened and listened in the downward direction. When, however, it had been still there for a long while, he looked behind, and saw Zarathustra standing trembling.
62:11 "O Zarathustra," he began, with sorrowful voice, "thou dost not stand there like one whose happiness maketh him giddy: thou wilt have to dance lest thou tumble down!
62:12 But although thou shouldst dance before me, and leap all thy side-leaps, no one may say unto me: 'Behold, here danceth the last joyous man!'
62:13 In vain would any one come to this height who sought HIM here: caves would he find, indeed, and back-caves, hiding-places for hidden ones; but not lucky mines, nor treasure-chambers, nor new gold-veins of happiness.
62:14 Happiness—how indeed could one find happiness among such buried-alive and solitary ones! Must I yet seek the last happiness on the Happy Isles, and far away among forgotten seas?
62:15 But all is alike, nothing is worth while, no seeking is of service, there are no longer any Happy Isles!"—
62:16 Thus sighed the soothsayer; with his last sigh, however, Zarathustra again became serene and assured, like one who hath come out of a deep chasm into the light. "Nay! Nay! Three times Nay!" exclaimed he with a strong voice, and stroked his beard—"THAT do I know better! There are still Happy Isles! Silence THEREON, thou sighing sorrow-sack!
62:17 Cease to splash THEREON, thou rain-cloud of the forenoon! Do I not already stand here wet with thy misery, and drenched like a dog?
62:18 Now do I shake myself and run away from thee, that I may again become dry: thereat mayest thou not wonder! Do I seem to thee discourteous? Here however is MY court.
62:19 But as regards the higher man: well! I shall seek him at once in those forests: FROM THENCE came his cry. Perhaps he is there hard beset by an evil beast.
62:20 He is in MY domain: therein shall he receive no scath! And verily, there are many evil beasts about me."—
62:21 With those words Zarathustra turned around to depart. Then said the soothsayer: "O Zarathustra, thou art a rogue!
62:22 I know it well: thou wouldst fain be rid of me! Rather wouldst thou run into the forest and lay snares for evil beasts!
62:23 But what good-will it do thee? In the evening wilt thou have me again: in thine own cave will I sit, patient and heavy like a block—and wait for thee!"
62:24 "So be it!" shouted back Zarathustra, as he went away: "and what is mine in my cave belongeth also unto thee, my guest!
62:25 Shouldst thou however find honey therein, well! just lick it up, thou growling bear, and sweeten thy soul! For in the evening we want both to be in good spirits;
62:26 —In good spirits and joyful, because this day hath come to an end! And thou thyself shalt dance to my lays, as my dancing-bear.
62:27 Thou dost not believe this? Thou shakest thy head? Well! Cheer up, old bear! But I also—am a soothsayer."
62:28 Thus spake Zarathustra.


Chapter 63. Talk with the Kings

[SS 1]
63:1 Ere Zarathustra had been an hour on his way in the mountains and forests, he saw all at once a strange procession. Right on the path which he was about to descend came two kings walking, bedecked with crowns and purple girdles, and variegated like flamingoes: they drove before them a laden ass. "What do these kings want in my domain?" said Zarathustra in astonishment to his heart, and hid himself hastily behind a thicket. When however the kings approached to him, he said half-aloud, like one speaking only to himself: "Strange! Strange! How doth this harmonise? Two kings do I see—and only one ass!"
63:2 Thereupon the two kings made a halt; they smiled and looked towards the spot whence the voice proceeded, and afterwards looked into each other's faces. "Such things do we also think among ourselves," said the king on the right, "but we do not utter them."
63:3 The king on the left, however, shrugged his shoulders and answered: "That may perhaps be a goat-herd. Or an anchorite who hath lived too long among rocks and trees. For no society at all spoileth also good manners."
63:4 "Good manners?" replied angrily and bitterly the other king: "what then do we run out of the way of? Is it not 'good manners'? Our 'good society'?
63:5 Better, verily, to live among anchorites and goatherds, than with our gilded, false, over-rouged populace—though it call itself 'good society.'
63:6 —Though it call itself 'nobility.' But there all is false and foul, above all the blood—thanks to old evil diseases and worse curers.
63:7 The best and dearest to me at present is still a sound peasant, coarse, artful, obstinate and enduring: that is at present the noblest type.
63:8 The peasant is at present the best; and the peasant type should be master! But it is the kingdom of the populace—I no longer allow anything to be imposed upon me. The populace, however—that meaneth, hodgepodge.
63:9 Populace-hodgepodge: therein is everything mixed with everything, saint and swindler, gentleman and Jew, and every beast out of Noah's ark.
63:10 Good manners! Everything is false and foul with us. No one knoweth any longer how to reverence: it is THAT precisely that we run away from. They are fulsome obtrusive dogs; they gild palm-leaves.
63:11 This loathing choketh me, that we kings ourselves have become false, draped and disguised with the old faded pomp of our ancestors, show-pieces for the stupidest, the craftiest, and whosoever at present trafficketh for power.
63:12 We ARE NOT the first men—and have nevertheless to STAND FOR them: of this imposture have we at last become weary and disgusted.
63:13 From the rabble have we gone out of the way, from all those bawlers and scribe-blowflies, from the trader-stench, the ambition-fidgeting, the bad breath—: fie, to live among the rabble;
63:14 —Fie, to stand for the first men among the rabble! Ah, loathing! Loathing! Loathing! What doth it now matter about us kings!"—
63:15 "Thine old sickness seizeth thee," said here the king on the left, "thy loathing seizeth thee, my poor brother. Thou knowest, however, that some one heareth us."
63:16 Immediately thereupon, Zarathustra, who had opened ears and eyes to this talk, rose from his hiding-place, advanced towards the kings, and thus began:
63:17 "He who hearkeneth unto you, he who gladly hearkeneth unto you, is called Zarathustra.
63:18 I am Zarathustra who once said: 'What doth it now matter about kings!' Forgive me; I rejoiced when ye said to each other: 'What doth it matter about us kings!'
63:19 Here, however, is MY domain and jurisdiction: what may ye be seeking in my domain? Perhaps, however, ye have FOUND on your way what _I_ seek: namely, the higher man."
63:20 When the kings heard this, they beat upon their breasts and said with one voice: "We are recognised!
63:21 With the sword of thine utterance severest thou the thickest darkness of our hearts. Thou hast discovered our distress; for lo! we are on our way to find the higher man—
63:22 —The man that is higher than we, although we are kings. To him do we convey this ass. For the highest man shall also be the highest lord on earth.
63:23 There is no sorer misfortune in all human destiny, than when the mighty of the earth are not also the first men. Then everything becometh false and distorted and monstrous.
63:24 And when they are even the last men, and more beast than man, then riseth and riseth the populace in honour, and at last saith even the populace-virtue: 'Lo, I alone am virtue!'"—
63:25 What have I just heard? answered Zarathustra. What wisdom in kings! I am enchanted, and verily, I have already promptings to make a rhyme thereon:—
63:26 —Even if it should happen to be a rhyme not suited for every one's ears. I unlearned long ago to have consideration for long ears. Well then! Well now!
63:27 (Here, however, it happened that the ass also found utterance: it said distinctly and with malevolence, Y-E-A.)
63:28 'Twas once—methinks year one of our blessed Lord,—Drunk without wine, the Sybil thus deplored:—"How ill things go! Decline! Decline! Ne'er sank the world so low! Rome now hath turned harlot and harlot-stew, Rome's Caesar a beast, and God—hath turned Jew!"
[SS 2]
63:29 With those rhymes of Zarathustra the kings were delighted; the king on the right, however, said: "O Zarathustra, how well it was that we set out to see thee!
63:30 For thine enemies showed us thy likeness in their mirror: there lookedst thou with the grimace of a devil, and sneeringly: so that we were afraid of thee.
63:31 But what good did it do! Always didst thou prick us anew in heart and ear with thy sayings. Then did we say at last: What doth it matter how he look!
63:32 We must HEAR him; him who teacheth: 'Ye shall love peace as a means to new wars, and the short peace more than the long!'
63:33 No one ever spake such warlike words: 'What is good? To be brave is good. It is the good war that halloweth every cause.'
63:34 O Zarathustra, our fathers' blood stirred in our veins at such words: it was like the voice of spring to old wine-casks.
63:35 When the swords ran among one another like red-spotted serpents, then did our fathers become fond of life; the sun of every peace seemed to them languid and lukewarm, the long peace, however, made them ashamed.
63:36 How they sighed, our fathers, when they saw on the wall brightly furbished, dried-up swords! Like those they thirsted for war. For a sword thirsteth to drink blood, and sparkleth with desire."—
63:37 —When the kings thus discoursed and talked eagerly of the happiness of their fathers, there came upon Zarathustra no little desire to mock at their eagerness: for evidently they were very peaceable kings whom he saw before him, kings with old and refined features. But he restrained himself. "Well!" said he, "thither leadeth the way, there lieth the cave of Zarathustra; and this day is to have a long evening! At present, however, a cry of distress calleth me hastily away from you.
63:38 It will honour my cave if kings want to sit and wait in it: but, to be sure, ye will have to wait long!
63:39 Well! What of that! Where doth one at present learn better to wait than at courts? And the whole virtue of kings that hath remained unto them—is it not called to-day: ABILITY to wait?"
63:40 Thus spake Zarathustra.


Chapter 64. The Leech

64:1 And Zarathustra went thoughtfully on, further and lower down, through forests and past moory bottoms; as it happeneth, however, to every one who meditateth upon hard matters, he trod thereby unawares upon a man. And lo, there spurted into his face all at once a cry of pain, and two curses and twenty bad invectives, so that in his fright he raised his stick and also struck the trodden one. Immediately afterwards, however, he regained his composure, and his heart laughed at the folly he had just committed.
64:2 "Pardon me," said he to the trodden one, who had got up enraged, and had seated himself, "pardon me, and hear first of all a parable.
64:3 As a wanderer who dreameth of remote things on a lonesome highway, runneth unawares against a sleeping dog, a dog which lieth in the sun:
64:4 —As both of them then start up and snap at each other, like deadly enemies, those two beings mortally frightened—so did it happen unto us.
64:5 And yet! And yet—how little was lacking for them to caress each other, that dog and that lonesome one! Are they not both—lonesome ones!"
64:6 —"Whoever thou art," said the trodden one, still enraged, "thou treadest also too nigh me with thy parable, and not only with thy foot!
64:7 Lo! am I then a dog?"—And thereupon the sitting one got up, and pulled his naked arm out of the swamp. For at first he had lain outstretched on the ground, hidden and indiscernible, like those who lie in wait for swamp-game.
64:8 "But whatever art thou about!" called out Zarathustra in alarm, for he saw a deal of blood streaming over the naked arm,—"what hath hurt thee? Hath an evil beast bit thee, thou unfortunate one?"
64:9 The bleeding one laughed, still angry, "What matter is it to thee!" said he, and was about to go on. "Here am I at home and in my province. Let him question me whoever will: to a dolt, however, I shall hardly answer."
64:10 "Thou art mistaken," said Zarathustra sympathetically, and held him fast; "thou art mistaken. Here thou art not at home, but in my domain, and therein shall no one receive any hurt.
64:11 Call me however what thou wilt—I am who I must be. I call myself Zarathustra.
64:12 Well! Up thither is the way to Zarathustra's cave: it is not far,—wilt thou not attend to thy wounds at my home?
64:13 It hath gone badly with thee, thou unfortunate one, in this life: first a beast bit thee, and then—a man trod upon thee!"—
64:14 When however the trodden one had heard the name of Zarathustra he was transformed. "What happeneth unto me!" he exclaimed, "WHO preoccupieth me so much in this life as this one man, namely Zarathustra, and that one animal that liveth on blood, the leech?
64:15 For the sake of the leech did I lie here by this swamp, like a fisher, and already had mine outstretched arm been bitten ten times, when there biteth a still finer leech at my blood, Zarathustra himself!
64:16 O happiness! O miracle! Praised be this day which enticed me into the swamp! Praised be the best, the livest cupping-glass, that at present liveth; praised be the great conscience-leech Zarathustra!"—
64:17 Thus spake the trodden one, and Zarathustra rejoiced at his words and their refined reverential style. "Who art thou?" asked he, and gave him his hand, "there is much to clear up and elucidate between us, but already methinketh pure clear day is dawning."
64:18 "I am THE SPIRITUALLY CONSCIENTIOUS ONE," answered he who was asked, "and in matters of the spirit it is difficult for any one to take it more rigorously, more restrictedly, and more severely than I, except him from whom I learnt it, Zarathustra himself.
64:19 Better know nothing than half-know many things! Better be a fool on one's own account, than a sage on other people's approbation! I—go to the basis:
64:20 —What matter if it be great or small? If it be called swamp or sky? A handbreadth of basis is enough for me, if it be actually basis and ground!
64:21 —A handbreadth of basis: thereon can one stand. In the true knowing-knowledge there is nothing great and nothing small."
64:22 "Then thou art perhaps an expert on the leech?" asked Zarathustra; "and thou investigatest the leech to its ultimate basis, thou conscientious one?"
64:23 "O Zarathustra," answered the trodden one, "that would be something immense; how could I presume to do so!
64:24 That, however, of which I am master and knower, is the BRAIN of the leech:—that is MY world!
64:25 And it is also a world! Forgive it, however, that my pride here findeth expression, for here I have not mine equal. Therefore said I: 'here am I at home.'
64:26 How long have I investigated this one thing, the brain of the leech, so that here the slippery truth might no longer slip from me! Here is MY domain!
64:27 —For the sake of this did I cast everything else aside, for the sake of this did everything else become indifferent to me; and close beside my knowledge lieth my black ignorance.
64:28 My spiritual conscience requireth from me that it should be so—that I should know one thing, and not know all else: they are a loathing unto me, all the semi-spiritual, all the hazy, hovering, and visionary.
64:29 Where mine honesty ceaseth, there am I blind, and want also to be blind. Where I want to know, however, there want I also to be honest—namely, severe, rigorous, restricted, cruel and inexorable.
